[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
# Project "Disaster" - Strategic Analysis & Evaluation
## Multi-Perspective Professional Assessment

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Analysis Date:** November 11, 2025
**Project Status:** Greenfield / Blank Canvas
**Core Theme:** "Several variations of same theme"

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
The project "disaster" represents a unique opportunity: a deliberately provocative name combined with a minimalist philosophy ("variations of same theme"). This analysis evaluates potential directions from technical, commercial, marketing, and strategic perspectives.

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Key Finding:** The name "disaster" is both a liability and an asset - it demands bold execution to justify the branding choice.

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Option A: Disaster Recovery & Resilience Platform
**Concept:** Multi-variant disaster recovery solutions for different industries
- **Variants:** Cloud DR, On-premise DR, Hybrid DR, Edge DR
- **Tech Stack:** Kubernetes, Terraform, Go/Rust backend, React frontend
- **Differentiation:** Industry-specific playbooks (healthcare, finance, retail)
- **Complexity:** High
- **Time to Market:** 18-24 months
- **Market Fit:** (Critical business need)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Option B: Chaos Engineering Framework
**Concept:** Testing system resilience through controlled failures
- **Variants:** Different chaos scenarios (network, compute, storage, data)
- **Tech Stack:** Python/Go, Kubernetes operators, Grafana integrations
- **Differentiation:** AI-driven failure prediction and automated remediation
- **Complexity:** Medium-High
- **Time to Market:** 12-18 months
- **Market Fit:** (Growing DevOps need)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Option C: Incident Management Multi-Tool
**Concept:** Unified platform with variants for different org sizes
- **Variants:** Startup version, SMB version, Enterprise version, Government version
- **Tech Stack:** TypeScript, Node.js, PostgreSQL, WebSockets
- **Differentiation:** Context-aware AI assistance during incidents
- **Complexity:** Medium
- **Time to Market:** 9-12 months
- **Market Fit:** (PagerDuty competitor)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Option D: Simulation & Training Platform
**Concept:** Crisis simulation environments (cyber attacks, natural disasters, system failures)
- **Variants:** Different scenario types and complexity levels
- **Tech Stack:** Unity/Unreal for 3D sims, Web-based for simpler scenarios
- **Differentiation:** Realistic multi-stakeholder simulations
- **Complexity:** High
- **Time to Market:** 24+ months
- **Market Fit:** (Niche but growing)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Option E: Multi-Framework Development Toolkit
**Concept:** "Same theme, different variations" - compare implementations across frameworks
- **Variants:** React vs Vue vs Svelte vs Solid, or Python vs Go vs Rust
- **Tech Stack:** Multi-language
- **Differentiation:** Educational platform showing framework trade-offs
- **Complexity:** Medium
- **Time to Market:** 6-9 months
- **Market Fit:** (Educational/developer tools)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
### Technical Recommendations
1. **Primary Focus:** Option C (Incident Management) - Best balance of market need and feasibility
2. **Secondary:** Option B (Chaos Engineering) - Emerging market with less competition
3. **Avoid:** Option D - Too capital intensive for initial launch

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Enterprise Market (Option C - Incident Management)**
- **Market Size:** $2.1B (2025), growing 15% YoY
- **Key Players:** PagerDuty ($650M revenue), Opsgenie (acquired), VictorOps (acquired)
- **Pain Points:**
 - Alert fatigue (62% of teams report)
 - Poor cross-team coordination
 - Lack of AI-assisted resolution
 - Expensive legacy solutions
- **Opportunity:** Mid-market gap ($50-500 employee companies)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**DevOps Market (Option B - Chaos Engineering)**
- **Market Size:** $850M (2025), growing 28% YoY
- **Key Players:** Gremlin, Chaos Mesh, LitmusChaos
- **Pain Points:**
 - Difficult to implement safely
 - Requires deep expertise
 - Limited scenario libraries
 - Poor integration with observability
- **Opportunity:** Democratization of chaos engineering

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
### Feature Prioritization (Incident Management Path)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### MVP Features (Month 1-3)
1. Multi-channel alerting (Slack, Teams, SMS, Email)
2. On-call scheduling and rotation
3. Incident timeline and documentation
4. Post-mortem templates
5. Basic integrations (5-10 monitoring tools)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### V1.0 Features (Month 4-6)
1. AI-powered incident triage
2. Automated runbook execution
3. Status page generation
4. Advanced analytics and MTTR tracking
5. Mobile apps (iOS/Android)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### V2.0 Features (Month 7-12)
1. Predictive alerting (ML-based)
2. Cross-incident pattern recognition
3. Automated remediation workflows
4. Enterprise SSO and RBAC
5. Compliance reporting (SOC2, ISO)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**"Several variations of same theme" Implementation:**

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
1. **Disaster Starter** - Individuals & tiny teams
2. **Disaster Team** - Small companies (5-50 people)
3. **Disaster Pro** - Mid-market (50-500 people)
4. **Disaster Enterprise** - Large organizations (500+ people)
5. **Disaster Government** - Public sector compliance

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
Each variant shares core codebase but has feature gates and compliance modules.

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**The "Disaster" Name:**
- **Risk:** Negative connotations, clients may resist
- **Opportunity:** Memorable, honest about pain points, conversation starter
- **Verdict:** - Bold but risky. Consider tagline to soften: "Disaster - So you don't have one"

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Visual Language
- **Theme:** Order emerging from chaos
- **Color Palette:**
 - Alert Red (#FF4444) - Urgent actions
 - Calm Blue (#2E5EAA) - Resolved states
 - Neutral Gray (#363B40) - Base
 - Success Green (#2ECC71) - Positive outcomes
- **Typography:** Clean sans-serif (Inter, SF Pro) for clarity under pressure
- **Iconography:** Simple, high-contrast for stressed users

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### UX Principles
1. **Stress-Optimized Interface:** Large click targets, clear CTAs
2. **Progressive Disclosure:** Hide complexity until needed
3. **Context Awareness:** Interface adapts to incident severity
4. **Mobile-First:** 67% of incident response starts on mobile
5. **Accessibility:** WCAG AAA compliance for high-stress scenarios

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
### Competitive UX Differentiation
- **PagerDuty Weakness:** Cluttered interface, steep learning curve
- **Our Advantage:** Clean, intuitive, AI-guided workflows
- **Innovation:** Voice-activated incident management for hands-free operation

- Basic analytics
- Mobile apps

- Up to 250 users
- Unlimited everything

- Unlimited history
- Advanced analytics & AI features
- Custom integrations
- SSO (SAML)
- API access
- **Revenue Target:** $9,875/month per customer (avg 50 users)

- Unlimited users

- Training & onboarding
- Professional services

- **Target:** Fortune 1000, regulated industries

 - Essential tool positioning (not nice-to-have)

- **Severity:** Critical
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Wrong recommendations during incidents = disaster
- **Mitigation:**
 - Human-in-loop for all critical actions
 - Extensive testing & validation
 - Gradual rollout with opt-in AI features
 - Liability insurance

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Risk 4: Security Breach**
- **Severity:** Critical
- **Probability:** Low-Medium
- **Impact:** Complete loss of trust
- **Mitigation:**
 - Security-first development
 - Regular penetration testing
 - Bug bounty program
 - Cyber insurance ($5M+ coverage)

## 10. ETHICS & SUSTAINABILITY ADVISOR'S PERSPECTIVE

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Ethical AI Use
**Challenge:** AI recommending actions during critical incidents
**Approach:**
- Explainable AI (show reasoning for suggestions)
- Human oversight required for destructive actions
- Bias testing across different infrastructure types
- Opt-in AI features (not forced)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Data Privacy & Security
**Considerations:**
- Incident data often contains sensitive information
- GDPR, CCPA, SOC2 compliance from day one
- Data residency options (EU, US, APAC)
- Zero-knowledge architecture where possible
- Clear data retention policies

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Sustainability
**Environmental Impact:**
- Carbon-neutral infrastructure (renewable-powered data centers)
- Efficient code (minimize compute waste)
- Remote-first company (reduce commute emissions)
- Offset residual carbon (partner with Stripe Climate or similar)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Accessibility
**Inclusive Design:**
- WCAG AAA compliance (lives depend on your product)
- Multi-language support (not just English)
- Screen reader optimization
- Color-blind friendly design
- Neurodiversity considerations (clear, simple UI for stress)

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Social Responsibility
**Labor Practices:**
- Fair compensation (top 25th percentile for market)
- Work-life balance (ironic for on-call product!)
- No forced on-call for employees (eat own dog food humanely)
- Diversity hiring targets
- Open-source contributions to community

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Concept A: "Embrace the Chaos"
**Visual Identity:**
- Glitch art aesthetics
- Dynamic, reactive logo (changes during incidents)
- Dark mode default (SREs work at night)
- Brutalist design philosophy

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Voice & Tone:**
- Honest, technical, no BS
- Gallows humor (respectful)
- "We've been there" empathy

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Tagline Options:**
- "Disaster. Managed."
- "Your 3am guardian"
- "Chaos. Controlled."
- "Break things. Fix things. Learn things."

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
#### Concept B: "Order from Chaos"
**Visual Identity:**
- Mandala/fractal patterns (order emerging)
- Calming blues and greens
- Clean, minimal interface
- Sacred geometry influences

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Voice & Tone:**
- Calm, confident, reassuring
- Zen-like wisdom
- "We've got this" confidence

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Tagline Options:**
- "Peace in the storm"
- "From disaster to discovery"
- "Clarity in chaos"
- "The calm in your on-call"

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]

- Comic book aesthetics
- Hero character mascot
- Bold colors, high contrast
- Gamification elements

[src: STRATEGIC_ANALYSIS.md]
**Voice & Tone:**
- Energetic, empowering

- On-call burnout prevention
- Stress monitoring

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
# 72-Hour Emergency Preparedness Guide
## The Critical First Three Days

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Timeline:** Immediate Emergency (0-72 hours)
**Priority:** Survival & Safety
**Portability:** HIGH - Must be grab-and-go ready
**Cost:** $$ (Most affordable preparedness level)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
1. [Why 72 Hours?](#why-72-hours)
2. [Water - 3 Days](#water---3-days)
3. [Food - 3 Days](#food---3-days)
4. [Shelter & Warmth](#shelter--warmth)
5. [First Aid & Medical](#first-aid--medical)
6. [Sanitation & Hygiene](#sanitation--hygiene)
7. [Communication & Information](#communication--information)
8. [Important Documents](#important-documents)
9. [Complete 72-Hour Kit Checklist](#complete-72-hour-kit-checklist)
10. [Special Considerations](#special-considerations)
11. [Free Resources & Downloads](#free-resources--downloads)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
The 72-hour kit is **the foundation of all emergency preparedness**. Here's why:

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
- **Emergency Response Time:** After major disasters, it typically takes 72 hours for organized emergency response to reach most areas
- **Evacuation:** Enough supplies to sustain you if you must leave your home quickly
- **Shelter-in-Place:** Enough supplies to survive at home if utilities are disrupted
- **Universal Need:** Applicable to nearly all emergency scenarios

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
- **Natural Disasters:** Hurricanes, tornadoes, earthquakes, floods
- **Fires:** Wildfires, house fires requiring evacuation
- **Power Outages:** Grid failures, ice storms, heat waves
- **Severe Weather:** Blizzards, severe storms, extreme temperatures
- **Evacuations:** Chemical spills, gas leaks, civil emergencies
- **Flooding:** Flash floods, dam failures, coastal surge

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
### Minimum Requirement
**1 gallon per person per day = 3 gallons per person for 72 hours**

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
- gallon for drinking
- gallon for cooking and hygiene

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Purchased Water (Easiest)**
- Commercial bottled water (check expiration dates)
- Store in cool, dark place
- Rotate every 6-12 months

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**DIY Water Storage**
- Food-grade water containers
- Clean 2-liter soda bottles (free!)
- Water bricks or jerry cans
- **Never use milk jugs** (impossible to clean completely)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Water purification for on-the-go:**
- Water purification tablets (iodine or chlorine)
- LifeStraw or similar personal filters
- Portable water filter (Sawyer, Katadyn)
- Bleach for purification (8 drops per gallon)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**FEMA - Food and Water in an Emergency**
- **URL:**
- **Type:** Free PDF
- **Content:** Water storage and purification guidelines

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**CDC - Making Water Safe**
- **URL:**
- **Type:** Free online guide
- **Content:** Emergency water purification methods

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
### Calorie Requirements
- **Adult Male:** ~2,400 calories/day = 7,200 calories total
- **Adult Female:** ~2,000 calories/day = 6,000 calories total
- **Children:** ~1,400-2,200 calories/day depending on age

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Must Have:**
- No refrigeration required
- No cooking needed (or minimal)
- Long shelf life (6+ months)
- Compact and lightweight
- Familiar foods (reduce stress)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Avoid:**
- Highly perishable items
- Foods requiring significant water for preparation
- Foods in glass containers (can break)
- Foods that increase thirst (very salty/spicy)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Ready-to-Eat Options:**
- Protein/energy bars
- Trail mix, nuts, dried fruit
- Jerky (beef, turkey, salmon)
- Peanut butter or nut butter packets
- Crackers
- Canned fruit, vegetables
- Tuna/chicken/salmon pouches
- Ready-to-eat soup cups
- Granola
- Instant oatmeal packets
- Cereal (can eat dry)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Comfort Foods (Important for morale!):**
- Hard candy
- Chocolate bars
- Cookies
- Instant coffee/tea

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Ready.gov - Build A Kit**
- **URL:**
- **Type:** Interactive guide
- **Content:** Emergency supply kit recommendations

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Red Cross - Emergency Preparedness Checklist**
- **URL:**
- **Type:** Free PDF checklist

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
### Core Body Temperature is Critical
- Hypothermia can kill in hours
- Hyperthermia (overheating) is equally dangerous
- Shelter is often overlooked in 72-hour kits

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**For Warmth:**
- Emergency blanket (mylar/"space blanket") - reflects 90% body heat
- Warm sleeping bag or wool blankets
- Extra clothing layers
- Hand warmers/heat packs
- Emergency poncho (wind/rain protection)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**For Shelter:**
- Tent or emergency tube tent
- Tarp (8x10 or larger) - incredibly versatile
- Paracord or rope (50-100 feet)
- Duct tape
- Emergency shelter pop-up

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Temperature Regulation:**
- Weather-appropriate clothing
- Rain gear
- Sturdy shoes/boots
- Hat (sun and cold protection)
- Gloves
- Sunglasses

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
### DIY Emergency Shelter (Tarp Methods)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**A-Frame Shelter:**
1. String rope between two trees (6-8 feet high)
2. Drape tarp over rope
3. Stake corners to ground
4. Can be built in 5-10 minutes

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**AMC - How to Build an Emergency Tarp Shelter**
- **URL:**
- **Type:** Free guide with illustrations

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Wound Care:**
- Adhesive bandages (various sizes)
- Sterile gauze pads (4x4)
- Adhesive tape
- Antibiotic ointment
- Antiseptic wipes
- Burn gel or aloe vera
- Tweezers
- Scissors

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Medications:**
- Pain relievers (ibuprofen, acetaminophen)
- Anti-diarrhea medication
- Antacids
- Antihistamine (allergies/allergic reactions)
- Any prescription medications (3-day supply minimum)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Tools:**
- Thermometer
- CPR face shield
- Disposable gloves
- Emergency blanket

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Personal Needs:**
- Prescription medications (7-day supply recommended)
- Glasses (spare pair)
- Contact lenses and solution
- Hearing aid batteries
- Medical equipment (glucose monitor, inhaler, EpiPen)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
Poor sanitation causes disease spread faster than almost any other factor in disasters. Even in 72 hours, basic hygiene is critical.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Personal Hygiene:**
- Toilet paper
- Feminine hygiene products
- Soap (bar soap lasts longer)
- Hand sanitizer (60%+ alcohol)
- Toothbrush and toothpaste
- Wet wipes/baby wipes
- Towel or washcloth
- Deodorant

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Waste Management:**
- Portable toilet or bucket with liner
- Plastic bags (heavy duty)
- Bleach or disinfectant
- Toilet paper
- Hand sanitizer

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Bucket Toilet (Cheapest DIY Option):**
1. 5-gallon bucket with lid
2. Heavy-duty trash bags
3. Kitty litter or sawdust (absorbs liquid/odor)
4. Toilet seat lid (optional, increases comfort)

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Commercial Options:**
- Luggable Loo
- Portable camping toilets
- WAG bags (waste alleviation and gelling)

- [ ] Complete inventory review
- [ ] Update emergency contacts
- [ ] Review family emergency plan
- [ ] Practice evacuation drill
- [ ] Rotate all stored items

- Emergency blankets

- First aid kit

- **URL:**
- **Type:** Free downloadable materials in multiple languages

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
1. **Power Outage Drill (12-24 hours)**
 - Turn off main power
 - Use only items from your kit
 - Practice cooking without power
 - Test lighting solutions

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
2. **Evacuation Drill**
 - Set a timer for 10 minutes
 - Grab your kit and evacuate
 - Drive to designated meeting point
 - Did you forget anything?

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
3. **Shelter-in-Place Drill (Weekend)**
 - Use only food/water from kit
 - Test sanitation solutions
 - Identify what's missing or inadequate

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**After each drill, ask:**
- What worked well?
- What was uncomfortable or difficult?
- What did we forget?
- What needs improvement?
- Are quantities adequate?

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Update your kit based on real experience!**

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Congratulations!** You're now better prepared than 90% of the population.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Next Steps:**
1. Test your kit (practice drills)
2. Create kits for vehicle and workplace
3. Begin building toward [2-Week Supply](TIMELINE_2_WEEKS.md)
4. Learn first aid and CPR (take classes)
5. Join community preparedness programs (CERT)
6. Help family and friends build their kits

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: Can I make one kit for my whole family?**
A: For shelter-in-place, yes. For evacuation, have individual backpacks for each person who can carry one (including kids).

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: How heavy should my kit be?**
A: General rule: No more than 20-25% of body weight. A 150 lb person should carry max 30-37 lbs.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: Where's the best place to buy supplies?**
A: Dollar stores, warehouse clubs (Costco, Sam's), camping stores, and online retailers. Don't overlook yard sales for containers.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: Should I buy pre-made kits?**
A: They're convenient but often expensive and may include items you don't need. Building your own is more cost-effective and customized.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: What about firearms for protection?**
A: This is a personal decision based on your comfort level, training, local laws, and situation. If you choose to include firearms, ensure proper training and legal compliance.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Q: How do I prepare on a tight budget?**
A: Build gradually (one category per month), buy items on sale, use coupons, repurpose containers, share costs with family members.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]
**Remember:** The best kit is the one you'll actually build and maintain. Start today, even if it's just storing 3 gallons of water. Every step forward is progress.

[src: TIMELINE_72_HOURS.md]

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
# TOPIC GUIDE: Food Storage, Nutrition & Preservation

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Comprehensive Food Preparedness Across All Timelines**

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**By Timeline:**
- [72 Hours](#72-hour-requirements) - Emergency supplies
- [2 Weeks](#2-week-requirements) - Extended pantry
- [1 Month](#1-month-requirements) - Short-term stockpile
- [3-6 Months](#3-6-month-requirements) - Medium-term sustainability
- [1 Year+](#1-year-requirements) - Long-term self-sufficiency

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**By Topic:**
- [Calorie & Nutrition Planning](#calorie--nutrition-planning)
- [Food Preservation Methods](#food-preservation-methods)
- [Storage Techniques](#storage-techniques--locations)
- [Rotation Systems](#rotation-systems-fifo)
- [Special Dietary Needs](#special-dietary-considerations)
- [Budget Building](#budget-friendly-strategies)
- [Free Resources](#free-resources--downloads)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**The Reality:**
- Grocery stores stock 3 days of food for local population
- Supply chains break in 24-48 hours during disasters
- Panic buying empties shelves in hours
- Average household has 3-7 days of food
- FEMA recommends minimum 2-week supply
- Long-term disasters require months of self-sufficiency

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What History Teaches:**
- Hurricane Katrina (2005): No food for 5+ days
- COVID-19 (2020): Bare shelves for weeks
- Texas Winter Storm (2021): 4+ days without supplies
- Supply chain disruptions (2021-2023): Ongoing shortages

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**You need:** Progressive food storage matching timeline preparedness

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Goal:** 3 days of ready-to-eat food requiring no cooking or refrigeration

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Target:**
- Adults: 2,000-2,400 calories/day 3 = 6,000-7,200 calories
- Children (4-8): 1,200-1,400 3 = 3,600-4,200 calories
- Teens (14-18): 2,000-2,800 3 = 6,000-8,400 calories

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Proteins (30% of calories):**
- [ ] Canned tuna/salmon (12 oz per person)
- [ ] Peanut butter (18 oz jar per person)
- [ ] Canned chicken/turkey (12 oz per person)
- [ ] Beef jerky (6 oz per person)
- [ ] Protein bars (6-9 bars per person)
- [ ] Nuts (almonds, peanuts) (8 oz per person)
- [ ] Canned beans (3-4 cans per person)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Carbohydrates (50% of calories):**
- [ ] Crackers (1-2 boxes per person)
- [ ] Granola bars (9-12 bars per person)
- [ ] Energy bars (6-9 bars per person)
- [ ] Rice cakes (1 package per person)
- [ ] Instant oatmeal (6-9 packets per person)
- [ ] Dry cereal (1 box per person)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Fruits & Vegetables (15% of calories):**
- [ ] Canned fruit (3-4 cans per person)
- [ ] Applesauce pouches (6-9 per person)
- [ ] Dried fruit (raisins, apricots) (8 oz per person)
- [ ] Canned vegetables (3-4 cans per person)
- [ ] Vegetable juice (3-4 cans per person)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Comfort & Variety (5% of calories):**
- [ ] Candy (chocolate, hard candy) (6 oz per person)
- [ ] Cookies/crackers (1 package per person)
- [ ] Instant coffee/tea bags
- [ ] Hot chocolate packets

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
- [ ] **Manual can opener** (P-38 style recommended)
- [ ] Disposable plates/bowls
- [ ] Plastic utensils (forks, spoons)
- [ ] Paper towels/napkins
- [ ] Trash bags (heavy duty)
- [ ] Hand sanitizer (for eating with hands)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Ultra-Budget ($30-50 per person):**
- Canned goods from discount stores: $15
- Peanut butter + crackers: $8
- Granola/energy bars: $12
- Canned fruit: $5

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Standard Budget ($60-80 per person):**
- Quality canned proteins: $25
- Variety of bars/snacks: $20
- Canned fruits/vegetables: $15
- Comfort foods: $10

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Premium Budget ($100-120 per person):**
- Freeze-dried meals (Mountain House): $50
- Quality protein sources: $30
- Organic/specialty items: $20
- Variety & comfort foods: $20

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Best Locations:**
- Plastic tote near main exit
- Backpack (portable for evacuation)
- Under bed in rolling bin
- Hall closet (accessible)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Temperature:** 50-70 degF ideal (avoid garage/attic)
**Light:** Dark location (prevents nutrient degradation)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Goal:** 14 days of food with minimal cooking (assumes some power/heat)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Target:**
- Adults: 28,000-33,600 calories total
- Plan for 2 meals/day + snacks

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Proteins (expand from 72hr):**
- [ ] Canned meats: 14-21 cans per person
- [ ] Peanut butter: 2-3 large jars per person
- [ ] Dried beans: 5 lbs per person
- [ ] Lentils: 3 lbs per person
- [ ] Canned beans: 12-18 cans per person
- [ ] Nuts/seeds: 2 lbs per person
- [ ] Protein powder: 2 lbs (optional)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Grains & Carbs:**
- [ ] White rice: 10 lbs per person
- [ ] Pasta: 5 lbs per person
- [ ] Instant rice: 2 boxes per person
- [ ] Oats: 5 lbs per person
- [ ] Flour: 5 lbs per household
- [ ] Crackers: 3-4 boxes per person
- [ ] Instant potatoes: 2-3 boxes per person
- [ ] Bread (fresh/frozen): 2 loaves per person

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Fruits & Vegetables:**
- [ ] Canned vegetables: 21-28 cans per person
- [ ] Canned fruit: 14-21 cans per person
- [ ] Dried fruit: 2 lbs per person
- [ ] Tomato sauce/paste: 6-8 cans per household
- [ ] Salsa/pasta sauce: 4-6 jars per household

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Fats & Oils:**
- [ ] Cooking oil (vegetable/olive): 32 oz
- [ ] Butter (shelf-stable or frozen): 2 lbs
- [ ] Mayonnaise: 1 jar
- [ ] Coconut oil: 16 oz (long shelf life)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Dairy Alternatives:**
- [ ] Powdered milk: 2 boxes per household
- [ ] Evaporated milk: 6-8 cans
- [ ] Shelf-stable milk: 12-14 cartons
- [ ] Condensed milk: 2-3 cans

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Seasonings & Cooking Basics:**
- [ ] Salt: 2 lbs
- [ ] Sugar: 5 lbs
- [ ] Honey: 32 oz (never expires)
- [ ] Bouillon cubes: 2 boxes
- [ ] Basic spices (garlic, pepper, etc.)
- [ ] Baking powder/soda
- [ ] Vinegar: 32 oz

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Beverages:**
- [ ] Coffee: 1-2 lbs
- [ ] Tea bags: 50-100 count
- [ ] Powdered drink mixes
- [ ] Hot chocolate: 1 box

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Minimum Required:**
- Camp stove with fuel (8-12 oz propane)
- OR fireplace/outdoor fire pit
- Pots/pans for basic cooking
- Matches/lighter

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 1:**
- Day 1-2: Fresh/frozen items (use first)
- Day 3-5: Pasta dishes with canned sauce
- Day 6-7: Rice and beans with vegetables

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 2:**
- Day 8-10: Oatmeal breakfast, canned soups
- Day 11-12: Instant potatoes with canned meat
- Day 13-14: Rice/pasta with canned proteins

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Budget Option ($150-200 per person):**
- Bulk rice/beans from warehouse stores: $40
- Canned goods (sales/discount): $80
- Cooking basics: $30

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Standard Option ($250-350 per person):**
- Quality pantry staples: $120
- Variety of canned goods: $80
- Comfort foods & variety: $50

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Premium Option ($400-500 per person):**
- Organic options: $150
- Freeze-dried additions: $100
- Gourmet items: $100
- Fresh frozen foods: $50

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Pantry Organization:**
- First In, First Out (FIFO) rotation
- Label with purchase dates
- Store by category (proteins, carbs, etc.)
- Keep inventory list on door

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Space Requirements:**
- 2 cubic feet per person minimum
- Standard pantry: Holds 2-4 weeks for 2-4 people
- Additional storage: Under bed bins, closet shelving

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Goal:** 30 days of food with variety and nutrition balance

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Target:**
- Adults: 60,000-72,000 calories total per person
- Balanced macronutrients across all meals

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**1. Grains (60 lbs total):**
- White rice: 30 lbs ($15-20)
- Pasta: 15 lbs ($10-15)
- Oats: 10 lbs ($8-12)
- Flour: 5 lbs ($3-5)
- **Why:** Long shelf life (10-30 years if stored properly), high calories, versatile

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**2. Legumes (40 lbs total):**
- Pinto beans: 15 lbs ($12-18)
- Black beans: 10 lbs ($10-15)
- Lentils: 10 lbs ($10-15)
- Split peas: 5 lbs ($5-8)
- **Why:** Protein source, 10-30 year shelf life, cheap

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**3. Proteins (30 lbs total):**
- Canned meats: 30-40 cans ($60-100)
- Peanut butter: 6 jars ($25-35)
- Canned fish: 15-20 cans ($25-40)
- Protein powder: 5 lbs ($40-60)
- **Why:** Essential amino acids, muscle maintenance

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**4. Fats & Oils (15 lbs total):**
- Cooking oil: 1 gallon ($10-15)
- Coconut oil: 5 lbs ($25-35)
- Shortening: 5 lbs ($8-12)
- Nuts: 5 lbs ($30-50)
- **Why:** Calorie-dense, essential fatty acids, 2-5 year shelf life

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Canned Goods (beyond 2-week):**
- [ ] Vegetables: 40-60 cans per person
- [ ] Fruits: 30-40 cans per person
- [ ] Soups: 15-20 cans per person

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Long-term storage requires preservation:**
- Fresh food lasts days to weeks
- Preserved food lasts months to decades
- Reduces waste and cost
- Provides variety year-round
- Essential skill for multi-year preparedness

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Canning:**
- Heat processing in sealed jars
- Kills bacteria/enzymes
- Creates vacuum seal
- Shelf life: 1-5 years properly stored

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Water Bath Canning (High-Acid Foods):**
- Fruits (pH < 4.6)
- Jams and jellies
- Pickles
- Tomatoes (with added acid)
- Equipment: Large pot, jars, lids, rings
- Cost: $50-100 startup

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Pressure Canning (Low-Acid Foods):**
- Vegetables (pH > 4.6)
- Meats
- Soups and stews
- Beans
- Equipment: Pressure canner ($100-300)
- Required for safety (botulism prevention)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
1. **Prepare jars:** Wash in hot soapy water, sterilize in boiling water
2. **Prepare food:** Follow tested recipe exactly
3. **Fill jars:** Leave proper headspace (1/4" to 1/2")
4. **Remove air bubbles:** Use plastic spatula
5. **Wipe rims:** Clean with damp cloth
6. **Apply lids:** Place new lid, screw on ring fingertip-tight
7. **Process:** Submerge in boiling water, process for recipe time
8. **Cool:** Remove, let sit undisturbed 12-24 hours
9. **Check seals:** Lid should not flex when pressed
10. **Store:** Remove rings, label with contents and date

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Pressure Canning Steps:**
- Similar to water bath
- Use pressure canner (not pressure cooker)
- Follow pressure and time guidelines exactly
- Adjust for altitude (add pressure/time)
- Never guess - use tested recipes only

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Safety Rules:**
- Always use tested recipes (USDA, Ball, Extension)
- Never reuse lids (rings are reusable)
- Discard unsealed jars (refrigerate and use within week)
- If jar looks/smells bad = THROW OUT
- Botulism is deadly - follow guidelines exactly

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Free USDA Resources:**
- Complete Guide to Home Canning (free PDF)
- URL:
- National Center for Home Food Preservation:
- Ball Canning website (free recipes)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What to Can:**
- Tomato sauce (from garden harvest)
- Fruit preserves
- Pickles (cucumbers, vegetables)
- Salsa
- Applesauce
- Meat (requires pressure canner)
- Bone broth
- Bean soups

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Cost Analysis:**
- Jars (quart): $12-15 per dozen (one-time)
- Lids (regular): $3-5 per dozen (annual)
- Rings: $3-5 per dozen (reusable for years)
- **Per quart canned:** $0.50-1.00 ongoing cost

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Dehydrating:**
- Removes 90-95% water content
- Bacteria/mold can't grow without water
- Shelf life: 1-10 years (depends on food and storage)
- Reduces weight by 75-90%

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Electric Dehydrator:**
- Cost: $40-300
- Best for: Consistent results, large batches
- Recommended: Excalibur ($200-300), Nesco ($60-100)
- Power use: 300-600 watts

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Oven Dehydrating:**
- Cost: $0 (use existing oven)
- Best for: Small batches, trying dehydrating
- Downsides: Less efficient, harder to control temp
- Set to lowest temp (140-170 degF), prop door open

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Sun Drying:**
- Cost: $0-20 (screens)
- Best for: Hot, dry climates (Southwest US)
- Downsides: Weather dependent, slow, pest issues
- Use: Herbs, hot peppers, tomatoes

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Solar Dehydrator (DIY):**
- Cost: $50-150 (build yourself)
- Best for: Off-grid, hot climates
- Free plans available online

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Fruits:**
- Apples (chips, leather): 6-12 hours
- Bananas: 8-12 hours
- Berries: 12-24 hours
- Pineapple: 12-18 hours
- Mango: 12-16 hours
- Peaches: 12-18 hours

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Vegetables:**
- Tomatoes: 8-14 hours
- Zucchini: 8-12 hours
- Mushrooms: 6-10 hours
- Onions: 6-10 hours
- Peppers: 8-12 hours
- Carrots: 8-12 hours
- Corn: 8-12 hours

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Meats (Jerky):**
- Beef: 4-6 hours
- Turkey: 4-6 hours
- Fish: 4-8 hours
- **Safety:** Pre-cook or heat to 160 degF

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Herbs:**
- Basil, oregano, thyme: 2-4 hours
- Cilantro, parsley: 2-4 hours

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
1. **Prepare food:**
 - Wash thoroughly
 - Slice uniformly (1/4" thickness ideal)
 - Pre-treat if needed (lemon juice for apples)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
2. **Arrange on trays:**
 - Single layer, no overlapping
 - Leave space for air circulation

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
3. **Set temperature:**
 - Fruits: 135 degF
 - Vegetables: 125 degF
 - Meats: 160 degF (finish at 145 degF)
 - Herbs: 95-115 degF

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
4. **Dehydrate:**
 - Check periodically
 - Rotate trays if needed
 - Test for dryness (no moisture when squeezed)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
5. **Condition (fruits/vegetables):**
 - Place in jar for 7-10 days
 - Shake daily
 - Redistributes moisture
 - Prevents mold

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
6. **Package:**
 - Vacuum seal bags (best)
 - Mason jars with oxygen absorbers
 - Mylar bags with oxygen absorbers

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
7. **Store:**
 - Cool, dark, dry location
 - 50-60 degF ideal
 - Check monthly for first 6 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Rehydrating:**
- Soak in water 15 minutes to 2 hours
- Use 1:1 or 2:1 water ratio
- Warm water speeds rehydration
- Save soaking water for cooking (nutrients)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Cost Analysis:**
- Dehydrator: $60-300 (one-time)
- Electricity: $0.03-0.10 per load
- **Per pound dehydrated:** $0.10-0.50 ongoing

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Fermenting:**
- Beneficial bacteria preserve food
- Creates probiotics (gut health)
- Increases nutrition and digestibility
- Shelf life: Weeks (refrigerated) to months (cellar)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Equipment:**
- Mason jars: $1-2 each
- Fermentation weights: $5-10
- Airlock lids (optional): $10-20 for set
- Large bowl/crock (optional): $30-100

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Vegetables:**
- Sauerkraut (cabbage)
- Kimchi (cabbage, radish, spice)
- Pickles (cucumbers, carrots, etc.)
- Fermented salsa
- Beets
- Green beans

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Dairy (if milk available):**
- Yogurt
- Kefir
- Cheese

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Beverages:**
- Kombucha (requires SCOBY)
- Water kefir
- Ginger beer

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Ingredients:**
- 1 medium cabbage (2 lbs)
- 1-2 tablespoons salt (non-iodized)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Steps:**
1. Shred cabbage finely
2. Place in bowl, add salt
3. Massage/pound 10 minutes (releases liquid)
4. Pack tightly into quart jar
5. Pour liquid over (should cover cabbage)
6. Weight down (keeps submerged)
7. Cover loosely or use airlock lid
8. Ferment at room temp 3-10 days
9. Taste daily (sour = ready)
10. Refrigerate to slow fermentation

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Safety:**
- Use non-iodized salt (iodine kills bacteria)
- Keep vegetables submerged (mold grows on surface)
- If mold appears: Skim off, cabbage below is fine
- Foul smell = bad (throw out)
- Fizzy/sour smell = good

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Benefits:**
- Probiotics (gut health)
- Increased vitamin C, K
- Preserves harvest
- Requires no electricity
- Adds variety to stored foods

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Root Cellaring:**
- Cold, humid storage (32-40 degF, 90-95% humidity)
- Extends life of fresh produce
- No processing required
- Shelf life: 2-8 months depending on vegetable

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Ideal Root Cellar Conditions:**
- Temperature: 32-40 degF
- Humidity: 90-95%
- Ventilation: Moderate air circulation
- Darkness: No light

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Option 1: Basement Corner:**
- Partition off NE or NW corner
- Insulate walls/ceiling
- Add vent to outside (cool air in)
- Add vent high on wall (warm air out)
- Cost: $100-500

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Option 2: Buried Container:**
- Old freezer or barrel (drainage holes)
- Bury at angle on hillside
- Vent pipe for air
- Insulate lid
- Cost: $50-200

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Option 3: Underground Cellar:**
- Dig 6-8 feet deep
- Concrete or stone walls
- Wooden shelving
- Proper venting
- Cost: $2,000-10,000

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Option 4: Garage/Shed (Cold Climates):**
- Insulated cooler/bin
- Monitor temperature
- Move indoors if too cold
- Cost: $50-200

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Long Storage (4-8 months):**
- Potatoes (dry first, 38-40 degF)
- Carrots (in sand, 32-40 degF)
- Beets (in sand, 32-40 degF)
- Turnips (32-35 degF)
- Parsnips (32-35 degF)
- Onions (dry, 32-40 degF)
- Garlic (dry, 32-40 degF)
- Winter squash (dry, 50-55 degF)
- Cabbage (32-40 degF)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Medium Storage (2-4 months):**
- Apples (32-40 degF, separate from vegetables)
- Pears (32-40 degF)
- Sweet potatoes (55-60 degF, dry first 2 weeks)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Short Storage (1-2 months):**
- Tomatoes (green, will ripen)
- Peppers

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Storage Tips:**
- Don't wash before storing (remove excess dirt only)
- Check weekly, remove any rotting items
- Store apples separately (emit ethylene gas)
- Layer root vegetables in damp sand or sawdust
- Hang onions/garlic in mesh bags
- Keep potatoes in dark (light causes green/toxic)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Freezing:**
- Stops bacterial growth at 0 degF or below
- Retains most nutrition (95%+)
- Shelf life: 6-12 months (quality), indefinite (safety)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Equipment:**
- Chest freezer: $200-600 (7-25 cu ft)
- Upright freezer: $400-1,000 (better organization)
- Vacuum sealer: $50-200 (extends life)
- Freezer bags/containers: $10-30

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Power Requirements:**
- Chest freezer: 100-250 watts (efficient)
- Upright freezer: 150-300 watts
- Backup power needed (generator, solar)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Vegetables (blanch first):**
- Corn, peas, green beans: 8-12 months
- Broccoli, cauliflower: 8-12 months
- Peppers (no blanching needed): 6-8 months
- Tomatoes (for cooking): 8-12 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Fruits:**
- Berries: 8-12 months
- Peaches, apples: 8-12 months
- Citrus juice: 8-12 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Meats:**
- Beef, pork: 6-12 months
- Poultry: 9-12 months
- Fish: 3-6 months
- Ground meat: 3-4 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Prepared Foods:**
- Soups, stews: 2-3 months
- Casseroles: 2-3 months
- Bread, baked goods: 3-6 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Blanching Vegetables (before freezing):**
1. Boil large pot of water
2. Prepare ice bath (large bowl)
3. Submerge vegetables in boiling water:
 - Leafy greens: 2 minutes
 - Broccoli: 3 minutes
 - Corn on cob: 7-11 minutes
 - Green beans: 3 minutes
4. Immediately plunge into ice bath (stops cooking)
5. Drain thoroughly
6. Package and freeze immediately

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Packaging:**
- Vacuum seal (best, removes air)
- Freezer bags (squeeze out air)
- Rigid containers (leave headspace for liquids)
- Label with contents and date

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Disaster Considerations:**
- Grid down = lost food in 24-48 hours
- Generator can extend (expensive)
- Solar backup systems ($3,000-10,000)
- Don't rely solely on freezing for long-term prep
- Freezing is supplemental to shelf-stable storage

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What is Freeze-Drying:**
- Freezes food, then removes ice via sublimation (ice vapor)
- Retains 97% nutrition
- Shelf life: 25-30 years properly stored
- Lightweight, rehydrates quickly

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Equipment:**
- Home freeze dryer: $2,500-5,000 (Harvest Right brand)
- Mylar bags + oxygen absorbers: $50-100
- Vacuum sealer: $50-200
- Electricity cost: $1-3 per load (24-40 hours)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**What to Freeze-Dry:**
- All fruits (berries, apples, etc.)
- All vegetables
- Cooked meats
- Complete meals (casseroles, pasta, etc.)
- Dairy (cheese, yogurt, ice cream)
- Eggs (scrambled)
- Desserts

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Cost Analysis:**
- Machine: $2,500-5,000 (one-time)
- Electricity: $1-3 per batch
- Packaging: $0.50-1.00 per batch
- **Commercial freeze-dried:** $15-25 per lb
- **DIY freeze-dried:** $3-6 per lb (including machine amortization)
- **Payback:** 200-400 lbs processed

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Pros:**
- Best nutrition retention
- Longest shelf life
- Lightest weight
- Best taste/texture when rehydrated

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Cons:**
- High upfront cost
- Slow process (24-40 hours per batch)
- Electricity dependent
- Requires learning curve

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Temperature:**
- Ideal: 50-60 degF
- Acceptable: 40-70 degF
- Every 10 degF increase cuts shelf life in half
- Avoid: Attics (too hot), unheated garages (freezing)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Humidity:**
- Ideal: 15% or less for dry goods
- Use: Desiccant packs, oxygen absorbers
- Avoid: Basements (moisture), coastal areas (high humidity)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Light:**
- Ideal: Complete darkness
- Light degrades vitamins (especially A, C)
- Use: Opaque containers, dark rooms, covered shelves

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Pests:**
- Prevention: Airtight containers, cleanliness
- Use: Food-grade buckets, Mylar bags, bay leaves (natural repellent)
- Check: Monthly for signs of infestation

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**5-Gallon Food-Grade Buckets:**
- Capacity: 35-40 lbs (depending on food)
- Cost: $3-7 each
- Source: Home Depot, Lowe's, bakeries (free used)
- Lids: Regular ($1-2) or Gamma Seal ($8-12)
- Use with: Mylar bags + oxygen absorbers

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Mylar Bags + Oxygen Absorbers:**
- Mylar bags (5-gallon): $1.50-3.00 each
- Oxygen absorbers (2000cc): $0.20-0.40 each
- Creates: Hermetic seal, no oxygen (prevents oxidation)
- Shelf life extension: 10-30 years for grains/beans

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**How to Package in Buckets:**
1. Place Mylar bag inside bucket
2. Fill with food (leave 2-3" at top)
3. Add oxygen absorber (2000cc for 5-gallon)
4. Seal Mylar with iron (clothes iron or hair straightener)
5. Let sit 24 hours (bag will shrink)
6. Snap on bucket lid
7. Label with contents and date

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Mason Jars + Oxygen Absorbers:**
- Quart jars: $12-15 per dozen
- Oxygen absorbers (300cc): $0.15-0.25 each
- Use for: Smaller quantities, easy access
- Shelf life: 5-10 years

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**#10 Cans (Commercial):**
- Large cans (108 oz / 3.2 qt)
- Pre-packaged freeze-dried foods
- Cost: $5-15 per can (empty) for DIY canning
- Shelf life: 25-30 years

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Challenges:**
- Limited space (500-1,200 sq ft typical)
- No basement/garage
- Temperature controlled (good)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Solutions:**
- Under bed storage (rolling bins): 40-60 cu ft
- Hall closet (floor-to-ceiling shelving): 20-40 cu ft
- Kitchen pantry (vertical organization): 10-20 cu ft
- Closet shelving (bedroom): 10-20 cu ft
- Furniture integration (hollow ottomans): 5-10 cu ft
- **Total: 85-150 cu ft = 3-6 month supply for 1 person**

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Best practices:**
- Vertical stacking (maximize height)
- Smaller containers (5-gal buckets fit in closets)
- FIFO rotation on shelving
- Temperature stable (climate controlled apartment)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Advantages:**
- More space (1,500-3,000 sq ft typical)
- Basement or garage
- Pantry room

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Storage locations:**
- Basement shelving: 100-200 cu ft
- Garage (climate controlled area): 50-100 cu ft
- Pantry room: 40-80 cu ft
- Under stairs: 20-40 cu ft
- Spare bedroom closet: 30-60 cu ft
- **Total: 240-480 cu ft = 1-2 year supply for family**

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Best practices:**
- Dedicated storage room in basement
- Heavy-duty shelving (1,000+ lb capacity)
- Climate control in garage (insulation)
- Organize by timeline (72hr separate from long-term)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Advantages:**
- Abundant space
- Outbuildings available
- Root cellar potential
- Can build dedicated storage

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Storage locations:**
- Basement: 200-400 cu ft
- Root cellar: 100-200 cu ft
- Pantry: 60-120 cu ft
- Outbuilding (insulated): 200-500 cu ft
- **Total: 560-1,220 cu ft = Multi-year supply for family**

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Best practices:**
- Separate building for bulk storage
- Root cellar for fresh produce
- Backup power for freezers
- Diversified storage (multiple locations)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Challenges:**
- Extreme temperature swings
- Long winters (access issues)
- Wildlife (bears, rodents)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Solutions:**
- Insulated storage building
- Interior basement storage (stable temp)
- Bear-proof containers (outside storage)
- Backup heating for storage area
- Stock extra (snowed in weeks at a time)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Best practices:**
- Store 2-3x normal amount (access limited in winter)
- Prioritize cold-stable foods
- Propane/wood heat backup for storage
- Wildlife-proof all outdoor storage

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Problems with poor rotation:**
- Food expires and wastes money
- Nutritional degradation
- Potential food safety issues
- Inefficient use of storage space

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Benefits of FIFO (First In, First Out):**
- Minimizes waste
- Ensures freshness
- Familiar with stored foods
- Identifies gaps in storage

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Step 1: Organize by Category**
- Grains (rice, pasta, oats)
- Legumes (beans, lentils)
- Canned vegetables
- Canned fruits
- Canned proteins
- Condiments & seasonings
- Snacks & comfort foods

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Step 2: Label Everything**
- Purchase date or expiration date
- Use painter's tape or permanent marker
- Label front of container/can

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Step 3: Front-to-Back Shelving**
- Newest items go in back
- Remove items from front
- Slanted shelving helps (cans roll forward)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Step 4: Create Inventory**
- Spreadsheet or app
- Track: Item, quantity, expiration, location
- Update monthly
- Flag items nearing expiration

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Step 5: Weekly Usage**
- Pull from storage for regular cooking
- Replace immediately after grocery shopping
- Practice cooking with stored foods

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Indefinite (if stored properly in Mylar + oxygen absorbers):**
- White rice: 30+ years
- Wheat berries: 30+ years
- Dried beans: 30+ years
- Sugar: Indefinite
- Salt: Indefinite
- Honey: Indefinite
- Hard liquor: Indefinite

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Very Long (10-20 years in ideal conditions):**
- Rolled oats: 10-30 years
- Pasta: 10-30 years
- Powdered milk: 10-20 years
- Freeze-dried foods: 25-30 years

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Long (5-10 years):**
- Canned vegetables: 2-5 years (quality), 5-10 years (safety)
- Canned fruits: 2-5 years (quality), 5-10 years (safety)
- Canned meats: 2-5 years (quality), 5-10 years (safety)
- Cooking oil (unopened): 1-2 years
- Nuts (vacuum sealed): 1-2 years

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Medium (1-5 years):**
- Dehydrated fruits: 1-5 years
- Dehydrated vegetables: 1-5 years
- Beef jerky (vacuum sealed): 1-2 years
- Coffee (unopened): 1-2 years
- Tea: 2-3 years
- Spices: 2-3 years (quality), longer safe

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Short (months to 1 year):**
- Brown rice: 6 months (1-2 years refrigerated)
- Whole wheat flour: 3-6 months (1 year frozen)
- Nuts (opened): 3-6 months
- Crackers: 6-9 months
- Chips: 3-6 months

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Rotate First:**
- Opened items (use within weeks to months)
- Items near expiration (within 6 months)
- Brown rice, whole grains
- Nuts and oils
- Baking mixes

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 1: Inventory Check**
- Walk through all storage areas
- Note items running low
- Flag expirations within 6 months
- Check for pest/moisture issues

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 2: Meal Planning**
- Plan next month's meals using stored food
- Create grocery list for replacements
- Plan recipes using near-expiration items

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 3: Cooking & Replacement**
- Cook meals using stored foods
- Replace used items immediately
- Put new items in back, old in front

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Week 4: Organization**
- Consolidate partially used items
- Reorganize as needed
- Update inventory spreadsheet
- Add new purchases to system

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Formula:**
- Stock: 2-week minimum, 1-month ideal
- Shelf life: 1 year unopened
- Rotation: Replace every 6-9 months
- Amount: 20-30 oz per day (depends on age)
- Types: Powder (longest shelf life), ready-to-feed (convenient)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Baby Food:**
- Jarred: 2 year shelf life
- Pouches: 12-18 month shelf life
- Amount: 2-4 jars/day (6-12 months old)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Needs:**
- Purified water for mixing formula
- Bottles, nipples, cleaning supplies
- Rice cereal (shelf-stable)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Budget:**
- Formula: $100-150 per month
- Baby food: $50-80 per month
- 6-month supply: $900-1,380 per infant

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Considerations:**
- Small portions (avoid waste)
- Soft foods (limited teeth)
- Familiar foods (picky eaters)
- Comfort foods important

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Recommended Storage:**
- Applesauce pouches
- Mac and cheese boxes
- Crackers, cheese crackers
- Canned fruits (in juice, not syrup)
- Peanut butter
- Soft granola bars

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Needs:**
- 4-8 years: 1,200-2,000 per day
- 9-12 years: 1,600-2,400 per day

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Considerations:**
- Higher activity = more calories
- Picky eaters (store familiar foods)
- Comfort foods critical (stress/trauma)
- Involve in food prep (builds skills)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Recommended Storage:**
- Favorite cereals
- Peanut butter & jelly
- Canned soups (kid-friendly)
- Mac and cheese
- Crackers, cookies, candy (morale)
- Comfort foods from regular diet

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Needs:**
- Males: 2,000-3,200 per day
- Females: 1,800-2,400 per day
- Athletes: Add 500-1,000 calories

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Considerations:**
- Rapid growth = high calorie needs
- Higher protein needs
- Snacking habits
- Can help with food preparation

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Recommended Storage:**
- High-calorie protein bars
- Jerky, nuts
- Pasta, rice (large quantities)
- Peanut butter (protein)
- Energy-dense snacks

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Needs:**
- Pregnancy: +340-450 calories per day (2nd-3rd trimester)
- Nursing: +450-500 calories per day

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Nutrition:**
- Folate/folic acid: Leafy greens (canned spinach), beans, fortified cereals
- Iron: Canned meats, beans, fortified cereals
- Calcium: Powdered milk, canned salmon with bones, fortified foods
- Protein: 70-100g per day
- DHA (omega-3): Canned fish (salmon, sardines, tuna - limit mercury)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Supplements:**
- Prenatal vitamins: 1-year supply minimum
- Iron supplement
- Calcium + vitamin D
- DHA supplement

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Hydration:**
- Extra water needs (100+ oz per day)
- Electrolyte drinks

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Budget Add-On:**
- Supplements: $150-250 per year
- Extra food: $500-700 per year

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Calorie Needs:** 1,600-2,400 (often lower due to reduced activity)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Special Considerations:**
- Difficulty chewing (soft foods)
- Reduced appetite (smaller, frequent meals)
- Medications (drug-food interactions)
- Chronic conditions (diabetes, heart disease, etc.)
- Dehydration risk (diminished thirst response)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Recommended Storage:**
- Soft canned foods (soups, stews)
- Oatmeal, cream of wheat
- Canned fruits (soft)
- Applesauce
- Protein shakes/powder (if appetite poor)
- Meal replacement drinks (Ensure, Boost)
- High-fiber foods (constipation prevention)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Medication Considerations:**
- Warfarin (blood thinner): Consistent vitamin K intake (avoid excess leafy greens)
- Diabetes: Low-sugar options, complex carbs
- Heart disease: Low-sodium canned goods
- Hypertension: Low-sodium options

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Gluten-Free (Celiac Disease):**
- Rice (naturally gluten-free)
- Corn products
- Quinoa
- Gluten-free pasta/crackers
- Beans, lentils
- Check labels (wheat in unexpected items)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Dairy-Free (Lactose Intolerance/Allergy):**
- Plant-based milk alternatives (shelf-stable)
- Dairy-free protein sources
- Calcium-fortified foods
- Nutritional yeast (B vitamins)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Nut Allergies:**
- Sunflower seed butter (peanut butter alternative)
- Soy nuts
- Alternative protein bars
- Check all labels (cross-contamination)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Vegetarian:**
- Beans, lentils (protein)
- Nuts, seeds
- Nut butters
- Whole grains
- Canned vegetables
- Protein powder (plant-based)
- B12 supplement

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Vegan:**
- All vegetarian items
- Ensure B12 supplementation
- Consider iron supplementation
- Omega-3 (flax, chia, algae-based)
- Fortified plant milks

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Principles:**
- Complex carbs over simple sugars
- High fiber (slows glucose absorption)
- Portion control
- Consistent carb intake

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Recommended Storage:**
- Steel-cut oats
- Brown rice (rotate more frequently)
- Whole grain pasta
- Beans, lentils (fiber + protein)
- Canned vegetables (low sodium)
- Nuts (healthy fats)
- Sugar-free items (limited)

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Avoid/Limit:**
- White rice, white pasta (store less)
- Canned fruits in syrup (choose juice-packed)
- Sugary snacks

[src: TOPIC_FOOD.md]
**Critical:**
- Extra medication supply (insulin, metformin, etc.)
- Glucose tablets
- Testing supplies

### Growing Your Own (Ultimate Budget Saver)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
# Water - Complete Resource Guide
## Purification, Storage, and Long-Term Sources

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Critical Priority:** Water is the #1 survival need
**Timeline:** Survival without water: 3-7 days maximum
**Minimum Need:** 1 gallon per person per day ( drinking, sanitation/cooking)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
1. [Water Requirements by Timeline](#water-requirements-by-timeline)
2. [Water Storage Methods](#water-storage-methods)
3. [Water Purification Techniques](#water-purification-techniques)
4. [Finding Water Sources](#finding-water-sources)
5. [Long-Term Water Solutions](#long-term-water-solutions)
6. [Water Conservation](#water-conservation)
7. [Water Quality Testing](#water-quality-testing)
8. [Free Resources & Downloads](#free-resources--downloads)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### 72 Hours (3 Days)
**Minimum:** 3 gallons per person
**Recommended:** 4-5 gallons per person
- 1 gallon/day drinking and food prep
- Additional for hygiene

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Storage:** Bottled water or home storage containers
**Purification:** Backup method (tablets or filter)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### 2 Weeks
**Minimum:** 14 gallons per person
**Recommended:** 20-25 gallons per person
- Includes basic hygiene and sanitation
- Some cooking water

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Storage:** Mix of bottled and bulk storage
**Purification:** Primary purification method required

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### 1 Month
**Minimum:** 30 gallons per person
**Recommended:** 40-50 gallons per person
- Full hygiene and sanitation
- Cooking
- Light cleaning

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Storage:** Bulk storage containers (55-gallon drums)
**Purification:** Reliable purification system
**Rotation:** Regular rotation schedule

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### 3-6 Months
**Minimum:** 90-180 gallons per person
**Recommended:** Renewable source + storage
- All household uses
- Limited laundry
- Dish washing

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Storage:** Multiple 55-gallon drums or water tanks
**Purification:** Multi-stage filtration
**Source:** Rainwater collection or well access

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### 1 Year+
**Requirement:** Sustainable water source
- 365+ gallons per person stored, OR
- Renewable source (well, spring, rain catchment)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Storage:** Large tanks (500-1,000+ gallons)
**Purification:** Comprehensive filtration and treatment
**Source:** Well, spring, rainwater harvesting system

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Short-Term Storage (2 Weeks - 3 Months)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
#### Commercial Bottled Water
**Pros:**
- No preparation needed
- Known quantity
- Portable
- Safe if sealed

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cons:**
- Expensive for large quantities
- Takes up space
- Plastic waste
- Can degrade over time

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** Check printed expiration date (typically 1-2 years)
**Rotation:** Every 6-12 months
**Storage:** Cool, dark place away from chemicals/solvents

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**2-Liter Soda Bottles (FREE!)**
1. Rinse thoroughly with water
2. Sanitize with 1 tsp bleach per quart of water
3. Rinse with clean water
4. Fill with tap water
5. Add 2 drops bleach per liter (if not chlorinated)
6. Cap tightly
7. Label with date

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** 6 months (rotate regularly)
**Cost:** Free
**Capacity:** 2 liters (0.5 gallon)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**5-Gallon Water Jugs**
- Camping/office water cooler jugs
- Food-grade plastic only
- Easy to transport
- Stackable

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** 1 year
**Cost:** $8-15 empty, $6-10 refilled
**Capacity:** 5 gallons (19 liters)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Water Bricks/WaterBobs**
- Stackable, space-efficient
- Durable
- Multi-purpose containers

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** Several years
**Cost:** $15-35 each
**Capacity:** 3.5 gallons per brick

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
#### 55-Gallon Drums
**Best for:** Basements, garages, long-term storage

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Setup:**
1. Purchase food-grade barrel ($50-100)
2. Clean thoroughly
3. Add water preserver treatment (optional)
4. Fill with clean water
5. Seal tightly
6. Label with date

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** 5 years with preservative, 6-12 months without
**Rotation:** Annually or when treating
**Space:** 55 gallons = 2 ft diameter, 3 ft height
**Weight:** 440+ lbs when full (plan floor strength!)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Important:** Once full, almost impossible to move

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
#### Water Storage Tanks (100-500 Gallons)
- Polyethylene tanks
- Can be indoor or outdoor
- May require stand or platform
- Various shapes (upright, horizontal)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Shelf Life:** Indefinite with proper treatment
**Cost:** $100-500 depending on size
**Maintenance:** Clean annually, add preservative

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
#### Large Cisterns (500-10,000+ Gallons)
- Underground or above-ground
- Concrete, fiberglass, or polyethylene
- Requires significant space
- May require permits

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Best for:** Rural properties, homesteads, multi-family
**Cost:** $500-5,000+
**Maintenance:** Professional cleaning every 3-5 years

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
#### Rainwater Harvesting Systems
- Collection from roof
- Gutter system to tank
- Filtration and treatment
- Can provide ongoing water supply

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Capacity:** Depends on roof size and rainfall
**Formula:** 1 inch of rain on 1,000 sq ft roof = ~600 gallons
**Cost:** $1,000-5,000+ for complete system
**Legality:** Check local laws (restricted in some areas)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Biological:**
- Bacteria (E. coli, Salmonella)
- Viruses (Hepatitis A, Norovirus)
- Protozoa (Giardia, Cryptosporidium)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Chemical:**
- Heavy metals
- Pesticides
- Industrial contaminants

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Physical:**
- Sediment
- Debris
- Turbidity (cloudiness)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Different methods remove different contaminants!**

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Boiling (Most Reliable for Biological)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Method:**
1. Filter or let sediment settle if water is cloudy
2. Bring water to rolling boil
3. Boil for 1 minute (3 minutes above 6,500 ft elevation)
4. Let cool before drinking

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Kills all bacteria
- Kills all viruses
- Kills all protozoa
- Does NOT remove chemicals
- Does NOT remove heavy metals
- Does NOT remove sediment (pre-filter)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** Fuel only
**Shelf Life:** N/A (method, not product)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Free Resource:**
**CDC - Making Water Safe**
- **URL:**
- **Content:** Official boiling guidelines

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Unscented, 5-9% sodium hypochlorite only**

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Method:**
1. Filter or settle cloudy water first
2. Add bleach:
 - Clear water: 8 drops (1/8 tsp) per gallon
 - Cloudy water: 16 drops (1/4 tsp) per gallon
3. Mix well
4. Let stand 30 minutes
5. Should have slight chlorine odor (if not, repeat and wait 15 min)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Kills most bacteria
- Kills most viruses
- NOT effective against Cryptosporidium
- Does NOT remove chemicals
- Does NOT remove sediment

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** ~$2-4/gallon (treats thousands of gallons)
**Shelf Life:** Bleach degrades 50% per year (replace annually)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Free Resource:**
**FEMA - How to Make Water Safe**
- **URL:**
- **Content:** Bleach purification instructions

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Types:**
- Iodine tablets
- Chlorine dioxide tablets
- Sodium dichloroisocyanurate (NaDCC)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Method:**
1. Follow package instructions
2. Usually 1 tablet per liter
3. Wait 30 minutes (or per instructions)
4. May need neutralizing tablet for taste

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Kills most bacteria and viruses
- Iodine less effective against Cryptosporidium
- Does NOT remove chemicals or sediment

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $8-15 for 50-100 tablets
**Shelf Life:** 3-5 years sealed
**Weight:** Very light, portable

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Popular Brands:**
- Potable Aqua (iodine)
- Aquatabs (chlorine dioxide)
- Katadyn Micropur

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Method:**
1. Use clear plastic bottles (not glass)
2. Fill with water (filter if cloudy)
3. Shake to oxygenate
4. Lay bottles on side in direct sunlight
5. Leave for:
 - 6 hours minimum (bright sun)
 - 2 days (cloudy)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Kills most bacteria
- Kills most viruses
- Requires clear water and strong UV light
- Does NOT remove chemicals
- Does NOT work in freezing temperatures

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** FREE
**Equipment:** Clear plastic bottles

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**LifeStraw/Personal Filters**
- Hollow fiber membrane
- 0.2 micron filter
- Drink directly from source

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Removes bacteria (99.9999%)
- Removes protozoa (99.999%)
- Does NOT remove viruses (usually)
- Does NOT remove chemicals

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $15-50
**Lifespan:** 1,000-4,000 liters
**Best for:** Personal use, camping, grab-and-go kits

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Popular Brands:**
- LifeStraw
- Sawyer Mini
- Katadyn BeFree

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Bag/reservoir with filter**
- Fill bag with water
- Hang from tree/hook
- Gravity pulls water through filter
- Collect in clean container

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:** (Depends on filter type)
- Removes bacteria (99.9999%)
- Removes protozoa (99.99%)
- Some remove viruses
- Some remove chemicals (activated carbon)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $100-150
**Lifespan:** 10,000-100,000 gallons
**Capacity:** 2-10 liters per fill
**Best for:** Family/group camping, base camp, 2-week to 1-month supplies

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Popular Brands:**
- Sawyer Products
- LifeStraw Mission
- Platypus GravityWorks

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Manual pumping through filter**
- Intake hose in water source
- Pump handle
- Filtered water to container

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:** (Depends on filter)
- Removes bacteria
- Removes protozoa
- Some remove viruses
- Carbon filters remove some chemicals

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $80-300
**Lifespan:** 1,000-10,000 liters
**Best for:** Faster than gravity, active pumping

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Popular Brands:**
- Katadyn Hiker
- MSR MiniWorks EX
- Survivor Filter

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Porous ceramic filter element**
- Can be gravity-fed or pressurized
- Often used in countertop systems
- Can be cleaned and reused

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Removes bacteria
- Removes protozoa
- Removes sediment
- Some models remove viruses
- Activated carbon version removes some chemicals

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $50-300
**Lifespan:** 1,000-5,000 gallons (cleanable/rechargeable)
**Best for:** Home use, long-term preparedness

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Popular Brands:**
- Berkey (very popular in preparedness community)
- Doulton
- ProPur

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Pressurized membrane filtration**
- Removes nearly everything
- Requires water pressure or pump
- Wastes water (3-5 gallons per 1 gallon produced)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Effectiveness:**
- Removes bacteria (99.99%+)
- Removes viruses (99.99%+)
- Removes protozoa (99.99%+)
- Removes chemicals
- Removes heavy metals
- Removes salts (can purify seawater!)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $200-500 for home system
**Maintenance:** Replace membranes annually
**Best for:** Long-term home preparedness, contaminated water

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cons:** Removes beneficial minerals, wastes water

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Multi-Barrier Approach (Best Practice)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Layer your purification for maximum safety:**

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
1. **Pre-filter:** Remove sediment (coffee filter, cloth)
2. **Primary treatment:** Boil, chemical, or filter
3. **Secondary treatment:** Different method (redundancy)
4. **Storage:** Clean, covered container

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Example:**
- Cloudy river water
- Pre-filter through cloth
- Filter through ceramic filter
- Add bleach or boil
- Store in clean container

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Safest (Still Purify!)**
- Springs
- Rainfall
- Dew collection

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Use with Caution (Purify!)**
- Streams and rivers (flowing water)
- Lakes and ponds (still water)
- Snowmelt

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Avoid (Last Resort Only)**
- Stagnant water
- Water with odor/color/foam
- Water downstream of industry/farms
- Floodwater

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**In Your Home:**
- Water heater tank (30-50 gallons)
- Toilet tank (NOT bowl) - top tank only if clean
- Pipes (drain from lowest point)
- Ice cubes in freezer
- Canned vegetables (liquid)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**How to Drain Water Heater:**
1. Turn off power/gas to heater
2. Turn off cold water supply valve
3. Attach hose to drain valve at bottom
4. Open hot water tap in house (allows air in)
5. Open drain valve, collect water

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Swimming Pools/Hot Tubs:**
- Can use for washing/sanitation
- Do NOT drink (chemicals!)
- Can purify through extensive filtration

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Simple Method:**
- Clean tarp or plastic sheeting
- Tie corners higher than center (creates basin)
- Collect in clean container
- Purify before drinking

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Permanent System:**
- Gutters on roof
- Downspout to collection tank
- First-flush diverter (diverts initial dirty water)
- Storage tank with overflow
- Filtration system

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Legality:** Check local laws
- Illegal or restricted in some states
- Some areas require permits

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Survival Technique (Low Yield):**
1. Tie cloth around ankles
2. Walk through dewy grass early morning
3. Wring out cloth into container
4. Repeat

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Yield:** Small amounts, labor-intensive
**Use:** Survival situation only

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Dug Wells (Shallow)**
- 10-30 feet deep
- Can be dug manually
- More susceptible to contamination
- Lower yield

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Drilled Wells (Deep)**
- 50-400+ feet deep
- Requires professional drilling
- More protected from surface contamination
- Higher yield

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $3,000-15,000+ depending on depth
**Considerations:**
- Requires electrical pump (or hand pump backup)
- Regular testing needed
- Maintenance required
- May require permits

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Hand Pump Backup:**
- Allows well use without electricity
- $300-1,500 installed
- Critical for preparedness

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Natural groundwater source**
- Must test for safety
- Can be developed for consistent flow
- Often requires land ownership or permission
- Free flowing water

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Spring Development:**
1. Identify spring source
2. Test water quality
3. Create collection box
4. Pipe to storage or use point

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $500-5,000 for development
**Benefit:** Gravity-fed, no power needed

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
1. **Catchment Surface (Roof)**
 - Metal or tile preferred
 - Avoid asphalt shingles (chemicals)
 - Clean regularly

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
2. **Gutters and Downspouts**
 - Keep clean (leaf guards)
 - Proper pitch for flow
 - Screen to prevent debris

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
3. **First-Flush Diverter**
 - Diverts first rain (washes roof)
 - Usually first 10-20 gallons
 - Critical for water quality

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
4. **Storage Tank**
 - Food-grade material
 - Covered (prevent algae, mosquitoes)
 - Overflow valve
 - Drain valve at bottom

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
5. **Filtration/Purification**
 - Sediment filter
 - Carbon filter
 - UV light or other purification
 - Multi-stage is best

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Collection Potential:**
Formula: `Roof Area (sq ft) Rainfall (inches) 0.623 = Gallons`

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
Example: 1,000 sq ft roof 1 inch rain 0.623 = 623 gallons

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Annual Example (40 inches rain/year):**
1,000 sq ft roof = 24,920 gallons/year

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Intake System:**
- Screened intake away from shore
- Multiple depths (seasonal variation)
- Pre-filtration

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Treatment:**
1. Sediment filter (removes particles)
2. Activated carbon (removes chemicals, improves taste)
3. UV sterilization (kills biological)
4. Storage tank

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Cost:** $1,000-5,000 for complete system
**Maintenance:** Regular filter replacement, UV bulb annually

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Drinking Water
- Use minimum needed
- Don't waste when purifying
- Share drinking vessels (family only)
- Drink enough to stay hydrated (don't ration to dangerous levels)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Washing/Hygiene
- Use wet wipes instead of washing (short-term)
- Two-bucket method (soapy/rinse)
- Reuse water when possible
- Wash only when necessary

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Cooking
- Use minimal water for cooking
- Save cooking water for washing
- Choose foods requiring less water
- One-pot meals

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Toilet
- "If it's yellow, let it mellow"
- Use greywater for flushing
- Consider composting toilet for long-term

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Greywater Reuse
**Greywater:** Water from sinks, showers, washing (NOT toilet)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Safe Uses:**
- Flushing toilets
- Watering plants (non-edible)
- Washing floors

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Unsafe Uses:**
- Drinking
- Cooking
- Watering vegetables

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
- Before drinking from new source
- Regularly from wells/springs (annually minimum)
- After flooding or contamination event
- If water appearance/taste/odor changes
- If anyone becomes ill

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Basic Tests:**
- Bacteria (coliform)
- pH
- Hardness
- Nitrates

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Comprehensive Tests:**
- Heavy metals (lead, arsenic, mercury)
- Pesticides
- Industrial contaminants
- Volatile organic compounds (VOCs)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Home Test Kits:**
- Basic bacteria tests
- pH strips
- Available at hardware stores
- $10-50

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Mail-In Laboratory Testing:**
- Most comprehensive
- Various test packages
- $50-300 depending on what's tested

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Professional Testing:**
- County health department (often free or low-cost)
- Certified laboratories
- Most accurate

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Rainfall Collection Calculator:**
Formula: Roof Area (sq ft) Rainfall (inches) 0.623 = Gallons collected

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
**Daily Water Needs Calculator:**
- Drinking: 0.5-1 gallon/person/day
- Cooking: 0.25-0.5 gallon/person/day
- Hygiene: 0.25-0.5 gallon/person/day
- Total minimum: 1 gallon/person/day
- Comfortable: 2-5 gallons/person/day

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Stored Water Smells Bad
**Cause:** Algae growth, bacterial contamination
**Solution:**
- Discard water
- Clean container with bleach solution
- Ensure containers are opaque or in dark location
- Add water preserver when refilling

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Water Tastes Like Plastic
**Cause:** New containers, non-food-grade plastic
**Solution:**
- Ensure food-grade containers only
- Rinse containers multiple times
- Add activated carbon filter
- Taste should dissipate over time

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Water Turns Cloudy
**Cause:** Mineral precipitation, contamination
**Solution:**
- If minerals, safe to drink but filter
- If contamination suspected, purify before use
- Check storage conditions

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Filter Clogs Quickly
**Cause:** High sediment in water
**Solution:**
- Pre-filter through cloth/coffee filter
- Let sediment settle before filtering
- Clean/backflush filter regularly

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]
### Can't Get Water Out of Storage Barrel
**Cause:** No siphon or pump
**Solution:**
- Install spigot at bottom of barrel (best)
- Use siphon pump
- Tip carefully (very heavy!)

[src: TOPIC_WATER.md]

Infrastructure
Resilience Planning
Framework (IRPF)
The Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has developed the
Infrastructure Resilience Planning Framework (IRPF) to enable the incorporation of security
and resilience considerations in critical infrastructure planning and investment decisions.
The IRPF is organized as follows:
Overview
Step 1. Lay the Foundation
Step 2. Identify Critical Infrastructure
Step 3. Assess Risk
Step 4. Develop Actions
Step 5. Implement & Evaluate
All Resources
Glossary

Infrastructure Resilience Planning Framework (IRPF)
5
February 2024 | Version 1.2
OVERVIEW
consider infrastructure in any existing or on-going planning process.
Communities can review the framework to determine where they are in
the planning spectrum and choose the guidance and resources that best
serve their needs.
Communities with limited time and resources may want to focus on the
infrastructure sectors that support critical functions, such as energy,
communications, transportation, and water and wastewater systems
initially, with the potential to expand later.
Conversely, communities with more time and resources could consider all
other critical infrastructure sectors deemed important and/or vital to the
continued performance of key social and economic functions integral to
the community or regional prosperity.
The IRPF helps users explore dependency relationships between
infrastructure systems to better understand infrastructure risk,
develop projects and strategies to address it, and identify funding and
implementation resources to take action.
Ultimately infrastructure resilience contributes to a more resilient
community, and can help develop and maintain a strong, safe, and
economically vibrant place to live and work. This can help form a self-
reinforcing cycle whereby increased social and economic resilience lead
to increased infrastructure resilience and vice versa.
0.3 THE INFRASTRUCTURE RESILIENCE
PLANNING FRAMEWORK (IRPF)
The IRPF is designed to be an easy-to-use framework for incorporating
critical infrastructure resilience into local, regional, and Tribal plans. It
is intended to help communities, regions, and infrastructure owners
and operators better understand critical infrastructure risk, identify
opportunities to enhance resilience, and inform policy and investment
decisions.
Step 1, Lay the Foundation. Communities define and scope the planning
effort, form a planning team to execute the effort, and review existing
data, plans, studies, maps, and other resources.
Step 2, Identify Critical Infrastructure. Provides guidance to communities
on how to identify and prioritize infrastructure and evaluate dependencies
among infrastructure systems.
Step 3, Assess Risk. Walks communities through the process of
conducting a risk assessment of critical infrastructure to include
evaluating vulnerabilities to threats and hazards, and consequences that
may result.
Step 4, Develop Actions. Provides guidance on the development of a
strategic action plan for addressing risk and enhancing infrastructure
resilience by identifying and prioritizing potential solutions.
Step 5, Implement & Evaluate. Focuses on incorporating infrastructure
resilience projects and strategies into community and regional plans and
processes for measuring success.

OVERVIEW
In many ways, the IRPF complements and supplements other resilience
guides and methodologies. For example, outputs from the IRPF can
inform Step 3, Risk Assessment, and Characterizing the Built Environment
of the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) Community
Resilience Planning Guide (CRPG). In addition, the infrastructure
resilience assessment process documented in CISA's Methodology for
Assessing Regional Infrastructure Resilience closely aligns with the
planning steps and guidance outlined in the IRPF.
0.5 RESOURCES FOR FUNDING OPPORTUNITIES
AND TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE
A key feature of planning is determining resource availability to develop
and carry out planning and implementation. The IRPF provides a
compendium of these resources in both a document and a user-friendly
matrix, outlining funding opportunities and technical assistance that can
help communities make planning a reality.

management, etc.) so that they present consistent strategies and
work together to reduce vulnerabilities to hazards.
